Kasneje oddane domače naloge se ne upoštevajo.\n\\end{minipage}\n}\n\\end{center}\n\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n\\textbf{1. Naj bo $a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$ za vsak $i \\in \\mathbb{N}$. S pomočjo matematične indukcije dokaži, da za vsak $n \\in \\mathbb{N}, n \\geq 2$, velja}\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_n) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_n\n\\]\n\\textbf{Vsak korak utemelji!}\n\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n\\section*{Razmislek in strategija reševanja}\n\nNajprej moram razumeti, kaj naloga zahteva. Dokazati moram neenačbo z metodo matematične indukcije.\nPogoji so:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item $a_i$ so pozitivna realna števila ($a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$).\n    \\item Neenačba velja za vsa naravna števila $n$, ki so večja ali enaka 2 ($n \\ge 2$).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nMoj načrt je, da sledim standardnim korakom za dokaz z indukcijo:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Baza indukcije:} Dokazati moram, da trditev drži za najmanjšo možno vrednost $n$, kar je v tem primeru $n\u003d2$.\n    \\item \\textbf{Indukcijska predpostavka:} Predpostaviti moram, da trditev drži za neko poljubno naravno število $k$, kjer je $k \\ge 2$.\n    \\item \\textbf{Indukcijski korak:} Dokazati moram, da če trditev velja za $k$, potem velja tudi za naslednje število, to je $k+1$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nPri pregledu neenačbe opazim, da je leva stran (LHS) produkt, desna stran (RHS) pa vsota. Ker so vsi $a_i$ pozitivni, bo pri množenju na levi strani nastalo več \"mešanih\" členov, ki jih na desni strani ni. To mi daje slutnjo, zakaj je leva stran večja.\n\nPoskusimo s preprostim primerom, da dobim občutek. Če bi bili vsi $a_i$ enaki, recimo $a_i\u003da$, bi neenačba postala $(1+a)^n \u003e 1 + n \\cdot a$. To je Bernoullijeva neenačba, za katero vem, da drži. To potrjuje mojo intuicijo, da je trditev verjetno resnična.\n\nSedaj se lotim formalnega dokaza.\n\n\\section*{Dokaz z matematično indukcijo}\n\n\\subsection*{1. korak: Baza indukcije}\nPreverimo, ali trditev velja za $n\u003d2$. Vstavimo $n\u003d2$ v neenačbo:\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2\n\\]\nZmnožimo člene na levi strani:\n\\[\n1 \\cdot 1 + 1 \\cdot a_2 + a_1 \\cdot 1 + a_1 \\cdot a_2 \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2\n\\]\n\\[\n1 + a_2 + a_1 + a_1 a_2 \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2\n\\]\nOd obeh strani odštejemo $1 + a_1 + a_2$:\n\\[\na_1 a_2 \u003e 0\n\\]\nPo predpostavki naloge velja $a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$ za vsak $i \\in \\mathbb{N}$. To pomeni, da sta $a_1 \u003e 0$ in $a_2 \u003e 0$. Produkt dveh pozitivnih števil je vedno pozitiven, torej $a_1 a_2 \u003e 0$.\nS tem smo dokazali, da trditev velja za $n\u003d2$. Baza indukcije je potrjena.\n\n\\subsection*{2. korak: Indukcijska predpostavka}\nPredpostavimo, da trditev velja za neko poljubno naravno število $k \\ge 2$. To pomeni, da velja neenačba:\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k\n\\]\n\n\\subsection*{3. korak: Indukcijski korak}\nDokazati moramo, da iz veljavnosti trditve za $k$ sledi njena veljavnost tudi za $k+1$. Dokazati moramo neenačbo:\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1}\n\\]\nZačnemo z levo stranjo neenačbe za $n\u003dk+1$ in jo preoblikujemo:\n\\[\n\\text{LHS} \u003d \\underbrace{(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k)}_{\\text{Del, za katerega velja ind. predpostavka}} (1 + a_{k+1})\n\\]\nUporabimo indukcijsko predpostavko. Vemo, da je prvi del produkta (v zavitku) večji od vsote na desni strani predpostavke:\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k\n\\]\nKer je $(1+a_{k+1})$ pozitivno število (saj je $a_{k+1} \u003e 0$), lahko neenačbo pomnožimo s tem faktorjem, smer neenakosti pa se ohrani:\n\\[\n\\text{LHS} \u003e (1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k)(1 + a_{k+1})\n\\]\nSedaj zmnožimo člene na desni strani:\n\\[\n\\text{LHS} \u003e 1 \\cdot (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) + a_{k+1} \\cdot (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k)\n\\]\n\\[\n\\text{LHS} \u003e (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) + a_{k+1} + a_{k+1}a_1 + a_{k+1}a_2 + \\dots + a_{k+1}a_k\n\\]\nPreuredimo člene, da dobimo obliko, ki jo želimo dokazati:\n\\[\n\\text{LHS} \u003e \\underbrace{1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1}}_{\\text{RHS za n\u003dk+1}} + \\underbrace{(a_1 a_{k+1} + a_2 a_{k+1} + \\dots + a_k a_{k+1})}_{\\text{Dodatni pozitivni členi}}\n\\]\nPo predpostavki so vsi $a_i$ pozitivni, zato je tudi produkt $a_i a_{k+1}$ za vsak $i$ od $1$ do $k$ pozitiven. Vsota teh pozitivnih produktov je prav tako pozitivna:\n\\[\na_1 a_{k+1} + a_2 a_{k+1} + \\dots + a_k a_{k+1} \u003e 0\n\\]\nTo pomeni, da je leva stran (LHS) strogo večja od vsote $(1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_{k+1})$ za nek pozitiven člen. Torej velja:\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1+a_{k+1}) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_{k+1}\n\\]\nS tem je indukcijski korak dokazan.\n\n\\subsection*{Zaključek}\nKer smo dokazali bazo indukcije za $n\u003d2$ in pokazali, da iz veljavnosti trditve za $n\u003dk$ sledi njena veljavnost za $n\u003dk+1$, po principu matematične indukcije trditev velja za vsa naravna števila $n \\ge 2$.\n\n\\end{document}\n```",

I will act as a scribe, translating your ideas into the formal structure of a proof. Then, we will discuss it.\n\nHere is the LaTeX code that represents your line of thought.\n\n```latex\n\\documentclass[12pt, a4paper]{article}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{geometry}\n\\geometry{a4paper, margin\u003d1in}\n\n\\pagestyle{empty}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\textwidth}{l @{\\extracolsep{\\fill}} r}\n\\textbf{FKKT UM, Matematika A} \u0026 \\textbf{1. domača naloga} \\hspace{1cm} \\textbf{2025/2026} \\\\\n\\end{tabular*}\n\n\\vspace{0.5cm}\n\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\textwidth}{l @{\\extracolsep{\\fill}} r}\n\\underline{Ime in priimek\\hspace{5cm}} \u0026 \\underline{K\\hspace{1cm} KI\\hspace{1cm}} \\\\\n\\end{tabular*}\n\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n\\textbf{1. Naj bo $a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$ za vsak $i \\in \\mathbb{N}$. S pomočjo matematične indukcije dokaži, da za vsak $n \\in \\mathbb{N}, n \\geq 2$, velja}\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_n) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_n\n\\]\n\\textbf{Vsak korak utemelji!}\n\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n\\section*{Moj miselni proces}\n\nNajprej sem poskusil razumeti, zakaj bi ta neenačba sploh morala biti resnična. Leva stran (LHS) ima enote, ki so ``zrcalna slika\u0027\u0027 desne strani (RHS) z dodanim +1. To mi je dalo slutnjo, da bo pri množenju na levi strani nastalo več členov kot na desni.\n\nDa bi preveril svojo idejo, sem si zamislil poenostavljen primer, kjer so vsi $a_i$ enaki, recimo $a_i \u003d a$.\nNeenačba bi potem postala:\n\\[ (1+a)^n \u003e 1 + n \\cdot a \\]\nLeva stran je produkt $n$ enakih členov $(1+a)$. To je $(1+a)(1+a)...$. Desna stran je $1$ plus vsota $n$ enakih členov $a$.\n\nČe poskusim to logiko uporabiti na originalni neenačbi, bi lahko rekel, da je leva stran približno $n$ krat nekaj v stilu $(1 + \\text{povprečje od } a_i)$, desna pa $1 + n$ krat $(\\text{povprečje od } a_i)$.\nČe so vsi $a_i$ enaki, dobim:\n\\[ n(1+a_i) \u003e 1 + n \\cdot a_i \\]\n\\[ n + n \\cdot a_i \u003e 1 + n \\cdot a_i \\]\nKo na obeh straneh odštejem $n \\cdot a_i$, dobim:\n\\[ n \u003e 1 \\]\nKer naloga pravi, da je $n \\geq 2$, ta poenostavitev drži. To mi da samozavest, da je trditev resnična. Sedaj jo moram samo še formalno dokazati z indukcijo.\n\n\\section*{Dokaz z indukcijo (na podlagi mojih idej)}\n\n\\subsection*{1. Baza indukcije: $n\u003d2$}\nPo navodilu je najmanjši $n$ enak 2. Vstavim $n\u003d2$ v neenačbo:\n\\[ (1+a_1)(1+a_2) \u003e 1+a_1+a_2 \\]\nRazširim levo stran:\n\\[ 1 + a_1 + a_2 + a_1a_2 \u003e 1+a_1+a_2 \\]\nOd obeh strani odštejem $1+a_1+a_2$:\n\\[ a_1a_2 \u003e 0 \\]\nKer je v navodilih podano, da je vsak $a_i$ pozitivno realno število ($a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$), vem, da sta $a_1 \u003e 0$ in $a_2 \u003e 0$. Produkt dveh pozitivnih števil je vedno pozitiven.\nTorej, baza indukcije drži.\n\n\\subsection*{2. Indukcijska predpostavka}\nPredpostavimo, da trditev velja za nek $n\u003dk$, kjer je $k \\geq 2$:\n\\[ (1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k \\]\n\n\\subsection*{3. Indukcijski korak: dokaz za $n\u003dk+1$}\nZdaj moram pokazati, da če drži za $k$, drži tudi za $k+1$.\nMoja ideja je, da uporabim enako logiko kot prej. Če je za $n\u003dk$ veljalo $k\u003e1$, potem bo za $n\u003dk+1$ veljalo $k+1 \u003e 1$, kar je očitno res, saj je $k \\ge 2$.\nTo je moja osnovna ideja, ampak poskusimo jo vgraditi v formalni dokaz. Začnem z levo stranjo za $n\u003dk+1$:\n\\[ (1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \\]\nVem, da lahko prvi del (do $a_k$) povežem z mojo indukcijsko predpostavko:\n\\[ \\underbrace{(1 + a_1)\\dots(1 + a_k)}_{\u003e (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k)} (1+a_{k+1}) \\]\nKer je $(1+a_{k+1})$ pozitivno, lahko zapišem:\n\\[ \u003e (1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \\]\nZdaj to razširim:\n\\[ \u003e (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) \\cdot 1 + (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) \\cdot a_{k+1} \\]\n\\[ \u003e 1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1} + a_1a_{k+1} + a_2a_{k+1} + \\dots + a_ka_{k+1} \\]\nTo je natanko to, kar sem hotel dokazati! Na desni imam $(1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_{k+1})$ in še kup dodatnih pozitivnih členov ($a_1a_{k+1}$, itd.), kar pomeni, da je leva stran zagotovo večja.\nTorej je trditev dokazana.       "text": "That is an absolutely brilliant question, and it gets right to the logical heart of why induction proofs can be so confusing at first. You are right, it feels like an unnecessary extra step, but it is the **most crucial step** for making the proof logical and not just a circular argument.\n\nLet\u0027s break it down using an analogy.\n\n### The Domino Analogy\n\nImagine you have an infinite line of dominoes, starting from domino #2. You want to prove that **all of them will fall over**.\n\nA proof by induction works exactly like this:\n\n1.  **Base Case (n\u003d2):** You prove you can knock over the first domino in the line (domino #2). You physically push it and it falls. Great.\n2.  **Inductive Step (The \"k\" part):** You need to prove that the dominoes are set up correctly. You don\u0027t knock them all over one by one. Instead, you make a general argument:\n\n    *   You pick a **random, single domino** somewhere in the middle of the line. Let\u0027s **give it a temporary name: `k`**.\n    *   Your **Inductive Hypothesis** is the *assumption*: \"Let\u0027s **assume** that domino `k` falls over.\"\n    *   Your **Inductive Step** is the *proof*: \"I will now prove that **IF** domino `k` falls, its momentum will **ALWAYS** knock over the very next one, domino `k+1`.\"\n\nIf you can prove both of these things, you have proven that all the dominoes will fall. Why? Because you proved #2 falls (base case). And your rule proves that since #2 falls, #3 must fall. And since #3 falls, #4 must fall. And so on, forever.\n\n### Why We MUST Use `k` (or another letter)\n\nNow, let\u0027s address your question: \"Why not just use `n` and `n+1`?\"\n\nLet\u0027s try to write the domino proof that way:\n\n*   \"Assume domino `n` falls over.\"\n*   \"Prove that domino `n+1` falls over.\"\n\nWhat is the variable `n` in this case? The original statement we are trying to prove is \"All (`n`) dominoes fall\". If you start by saying \"Assume domino `n` falls over\", you are **assuming the very thing you are trying to prove!**\n\nThis is a catastrophic logical error called **circular reasoning**. It\u0027s like saying:\n*   \"I want to prove that all dogs can talk.\"\n*   \"My proof: First, assume all dogs can talk. Therefore, my dog (`n`) can talk. If my dog can talk, the next dog (`n+1`) can probably talk too. QED.\"\n\nIt makes no sense.\n\n### The Roles of the Variables\n\nUsing `k` solves this problem by creating a clear logical separation.\n\n*   `n`: Is the variable for the **universal statement** you are trying to prove. \"This statement is true for **ALL** `n` ≥ 2.\"\n*   `k`: Is a **placeholder for a single, specific instance**. It is not \"all numbers\"; it is \"one number\". When we write \"Assume the statement is true for `n\u003dk`,\" we are saying, \"Let\u0027s stop talking about ALL numbers for a moment. Let\u0027s hypothetically pick ONE number, call it `k`, and assume our statement is true *just for that one, single number*.\"\n*   `k+1`: Is the next specific instance we are testing. The goal is to prove that if our hypothesis for the *single case `k`* is true, then it must also be true for the *single case `k+1`*.\n\nBy proving the `k` implies `k+1` step, you create a logical chain. The base case (`n\u003d2`) is the first link in the chain, and the inductive step is the rule that says the chain goes on forever.\n\n**In short: We use `k` to avoid assuming the conclusion. It allows us to say \"IF it\u0027s true for this one specific thing, THEN it must be true for the next thing,\" which is a powerful logical argument, instead of saying \"Let\u0027s assume it\u0027s true for everything, therefore it\u0027s true for everything,\" which is just nonsense.**",

      "text": "That is a fantastic, deep question. It shows you\u0027re thinking critically about what each part of the problem statement means.\n\nThe answer is: **You do not prove that `a_{i+1}` is bigger than `a_i`. It does not have to be, and the proof works even if it isn\u0027t!**\n\nThis is a very common point of confusion. Let\u0027s separate the two key variables:\n\n*   **`n`**: This is the variable for the induction. It represents the **number of terms** you are multiplying and adding. When `n` increases to `n+1`, you are simply making the equation *longer* by adding one more term to the sequence.\n*   **`a_i`**: This is the **value** of the term at position `i`. The problem states that `a_i ∈ ℝ⁺` (it\u0027s a positive real number). It says **absolutely nothing** about the relationship between the terms themselves.\n\nThe sequence `a_1, a_2, a_3, ...` could be anything, as long as the numbers are positive. For example, all of these are perfectly valid sequences for this problem:\n\n*   **Increasing:** `a_1 \u003d 1, a_2 \u003d 2, a_3 \u003d 3, ...`\n*   **Decreasing:** `a_1 \u003d 100, a_2 \u003d 10, a_3 \u003d 1, a_4 \u003d 0.5, ...`\n*   **Random:** `a_1 \u003d 5, a_2 \u003d 0.1, a_3 \u003d 20, a_4 \u003d 0.003, ...`\n*   **Constant:** `a_1 \u003d 7, a_2 \u003d 7, a_3 \u003d 7, ...`\n\nYour proof has to be general enough to work for **all** of these cases.\n\n### So, what does the increase from `n\u003dk` to `n\u003dk+1` actually do?\n\nIt doesn\u0027t change the values of `a_1, a_2, ..., a_k`. It simply means we are now considering an expression that is one term longer.\n\nLet\u0027s look at the inductive step again, but this time focus on the new term, `a_{k+1}`.\n\nWe proved this:\n`LHS \u003e 1 + a_1 + ... + a_k + a_{k+1} + (a_1a_{k+1} + a_2a_{k+1} + ... + a_ka_{k+1})`\n\nNow, let\u0027s think about what we needed to know about `a_{k+1}` to get here.\nWe did *not* need to know if `a_{k+1} \u003e a_k`.\n\nHere is what we **did** need to know, and it\u0027s the only thing we needed to know:\n**We needed to know that `a_{k+1}` is positive.**\n\nWhy?\n\n1.  When we multiplied the inequality by `(1 + a_{k+1})`, we needed to be sure that `(1 + a_{k+1})` was a positive number so that the direction of the `\u003e` sign wouldn\u0027t flip. Since `a_{k+1} \u003e 0`, we know `1 + a_{k+1} \u003e 1`, so it\u0027s definitely positive.\n2.  The \"extra stuff\" at the end, `(a_1a_{k+1} + a_2a_{k+1} + ... + a_ka_{k+1})`, had to be a positive value. Since all the previous `a_i` are positive and `a_{k+1}` is also positive, every single one of those products is positive, and their sum is therefore positive.\n\n**Analogy:** Imagine you have a bag of money.\n\n*   The induction hypothesis says: \"At day `k`, I have more than `(1 + a_1 + ... + a_k)` dollars.\"\n*   The induction step for day `k+1` involves a new transaction, `a_{k+1}`.\n*   The problem does **not** say that today\u0027s transaction (`a_{k+1}`) has to be bigger than yesterday\u0027s (`a_k`). Maybe yesterday you found $100 and today you only found $5.\n*   The only thing that matters is that `a_{k+1}` is a **positive** amount of money. Because you are adding *more* money to the system (both in the sum and especially in the cross-products), the final amount will be bigger than just the simple sum of all the daily transactions.\n\n**Conclusion:** Don\u0027t worry about the relative size of the `a_i` terms. The logic of the induction only depends on them being positive. The increase in `n` simply means you\u0027re adding one more positive term to the chain, which is enough to keep the inequality true.",

It\u0027s the starting point, the first domino you knock over.\n*   **In our problem:** The problem says `n ≥ 2`. So the first possible value is `n\u003d2`.\n*   **Your Action:** You correctly did this. You plugged `n\u003d2` into the inequality and proved that `(1+a₁)(1+a₂) \u003e 1+a₁+a₂` is a true statement.\n\n**The job of the base case is now finished. We have established a single, concrete fact: The statement is true for n\u003d2.**\n\n---\n\n### Part 2: The Inductive Step\n\n*   **Its Job:** To prove a **general rule** that says: **IF** the statement is true for *any* random step, **THEN** it must also be true for the very next step. This is the engine that drives the logic from one number to the next. It\u0027s the proof that the dominoes are spaced correctly so that any falling domino will knock over the next one.\n*   **Your Action:** This is where the `k` and `k+1` come in. You cannot use specific numbers here, because you need to prove a *general* rule.\n\nLet\u0027s look at your suggestion: \"our induction step as `n+1 \u003d 2`\".\n\nIf `n+1 \u003d 2`, that would mean `n\u003d1`. This doesn\u0027t work for two reasons:\n1.  The problem states `n ≥ 2`, so `n\u003d1` is not even allowed.\n2.  More importantly, the induction step isn\u0027t about proving a specific number like 2. Its job is to prove the connection between a number `k` and the number `k+1`.\n\n### The Correct Logic of the Inductive Step\n\nThe inductive step is a self-contained \"if-then\" proof:\n\n1.  **The Assumption (The \"IF\" part):** We start by saying, \"Let\u0027s assume we already know the statement is true for some general number `k` (where `k` is at least our base case, so `k ≥ 2`).\"\n    *   This is where we write down the *Inductive Hypothesis*:\n        `(1 + a₁)...(1 + aₖ) \u003e 1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ`\n    *   Think of this as: \"Okay, we know for a fact it works for `n\u003d2`. Let\u0027s just pretend for a moment that we\u0027ve already proven it all the way up to `n\u003d100`. So we assume it\u0027s true for `k\u003d100`.\"\n\n2.  **The Goal (The \"THEN\" part):** Now, using only our assumption from step 1, we must prove that the statement is also true for the very next number, `k+1`.\n    *   This is where we must prove:\n        `(1 + a₁)...(1 + aₖ)(1 + aₖ₊₁) \u003e 1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ + aₖ₊₁`\n    *   Following the analogy: \"Using the fact that it works for `n\u003d100`, can we prove it must work for `n\u003d101`?\"\n\nIf we can successfully prove this \"if-then\" statement, we have created a logical chain.\n\n*   We know it\u0027s true for `n\u003d2` (from the base case).\n*   Our inductive step proves that since it\u0027s true for `n\u003d2`, it must be true for `n\u003d2+1\u003d3`.\n*   Now we know it\u0027s true for `n\u003d3`. Our inductive step proves that since it\u0027s true for `n\u003d3`, it must be true for `n\u003d3+1\u003d4`.\n*   ...and so on, forever.\n\n**In summary: The base case establishes a starting point. The inductive step establishes a rule for getting from any point to the next point. You cannot combine them.**",

      "text": "This is the most important question in understanding induction. You have hit on the absolute core of the entire concept.\n\nThe answer is **no**, and this is the reason induction is such a special and powerful form of proof.\n\nJust because a pattern holds for the first step and the next step, you have **no guarantee** that it will hold for all future steps. Mathematical history is filled with patterns that seemed true for a long time and then suddenly broke.\n\n### A Simple Counterexample\n\nLet\u0027s propose a new statement to prove:\n\n**\"For all integers `n ≥ 2`, the number `n` is less than 4.\"**\n\nLet\u0027s try to prove this using your proposed method:\n\n1.  **Base Case (`n\u003d2`):** Is `2 \u003c 4`? Yes, it is. The statement is true for the base case.\n2.  **The Next Step (`n\u003d3`):** Is `3 \u003c 4`? Yes, it is. The statement is true for the next step.\n\nSo, we\u0027ve proven it for our baseline (`n\u003d2`) and the very next step (`n\u003d3`). Is the statement now proven for all `n ≥ 2`?\n\nOf course not! The moment we check `n\u003d4`, the statement `4 \u003c 4` is false. The pattern broke.\n\n### What Was Missing?\n\nWhat was missing was the **logical bridge** that guarantees you can get from one step to the next, *no matter where you are*.\n\nProving the `n\u003d3` case based on the `n\u003d2` case (as we did in the previous conversation) is an example of that bridge. But you didn\u0027t just do that. You are asking if simply proving `P(2)` is true and `P(3)` is true, as two separate facts, is enough. The answer is no.\n\nLet\u0027s go back to the dominoes. Your proposal is equivalent to this:\n\n1.  I push over domino #2. It falls. (Base Case `P(2)` is true).\n2.  I walk over and separately push over domino #3. It also falls. (`P(3)` is true).\n\nHave I proven that all the dominoes will fall? No. I haven\u0027t proven any connection between them. I don\u0027t know if #3 fell *because* #2 fell. And I have absolutely no idea if #4 will fall.\n\n### The Power of the Inductive Step (`k -\u003e k+1`)\n\nThe inductive step is the proof of the **connection**. It\u0027s the proof that the dominoes are spaced correctly.\n\nWhen you prove that **IF** the statement is true for a general `k`, **THEN** it must be true for `k+1`, you are proving that the connection holds everywhere, for the entire infinite line of dominoes.\n\n*   It proves that `2 -\u003e 3`.\n*   It proves that `3 -\u003e 4`.\n*   It proves that `4 -\u003e 5`.\n*   It proves that `5,487 -\u003e 5,488`.\n\nYou prove this general rule of connection **once**. Then, when you combine that rule with the fact that the first domino falls (the base case), the conclusion that *all* of them must fall becomes inescapable.\n\n**Conclusion:** A proof by induction requires two essential ingredients, and you cannot skip either one:\n\n1.  **A Starting Point (Base Case):** Proof that the statement is true for the first value (e.g., `P(2)`).\n2.  **A Guarantee of Continuation (Inductive Step):** Proof that `P(k) -\u003e P(k+1)` for any `k`.\n\nProving `P(2)` and `P(3)` separately only gives you two starting points, with no guarantee of continuation.",

      "text": "Excellent idea. You are exactly right—if the algebra is confusing, the best thing to do is to switch to a simpler, more familiar problem to master the *steps* of induction. Once you are confident with the steps, the more complex algebra in the original problem will make much more sense.\n\nYou\u0027ve picked the most famous example for induction!\n\nThere\u0027s a small typo in your formula. The sum `1 + 2 + 3 + ... + n` is equal to `n(n+1)/2`. (What you wrote, `n(n+1)/n`, simplifies to `n+1`, which isn\u0027t correct. For example, for n\u003d3, 1+2+3\u003d6, but n+1\u003d4).\n\nLet\u0027s prove the correct formula using the three steps: **`1 + 2 + ... + n \u003d n(n+1)/2`**\n\n---\n\n### 1. Base Case (n\u003d1)\n\nWe must first prove the formula is true for the smallest possible value, `n\u003d1`.\n\n*   **Left-Hand Side (LHS):** The sum is just `1`.\n*   **Right-Hand Side (RHS):** The formula is `1(1+1)/2 \u003d 1(2)/2 \u003d 1`.\n\nSince `1 \u003d 1`, the base case is true. The formula works for `n\u003d1`.\n\n### 2. Inductive Hypothesis (Assume it\u0027s true for n\u003dk)\n\nNow, we don\u0027t jump to the next number. Instead, we make a general assumption. We assume the formula is true for some arbitrary positive integer `k`.\n\nWe **assume** this is a true statement:\n`1 + 2 + ... + k \u003d k(k+1)/2`\n\nFor now, this is just a tool we are putting in our toolbox. We will use this tool in the next step.\n\n### 3. Inductive Step (Prove it\u0027s true for n\u003dk+1)\n\nThis is the most important part. Our goal is to prove that **if** our assumption for `k` is true, then the formula must also be true for the very next number, `k+1`.\n\n**Our Goal:** We need to prove this:\n`1 + 2 + ... + k + (k+1) \u003d (k+1)((k+1)+1)/2`\nwhich simplifies to:\n`1 + 2 + ... + k + (k+1) \u003d (k+1)(k+2)/2`\n\n**The Proof:**\nLet\u0027s start with the Left-Hand Side (LHS) of our goal, because it\u0027s more complicated.\n`LHS \u003d 1 + 2 + ... + k + (k+1)`\n\nNow, look closely. The first part of this expression, `1 + 2 + ... + k`, is exactly the left side of our **Inductive Hypothesis** (the tool in our toolbox).\n\nSo, we can **replace** `1 + 2 + ... + k` with `k(k+1)/2`.\n\n`LHS \u003d [k(k+1)/2] + (k+1)`\n\nNow our job is pure algebra. We need to show that this expression is the same as the RHS of our goal.\nLet\u0027s find a common denominator:\n`LHS \u003d k(k+1)/2 + 2(k+1)/2`\n\nCombine the fractions:\n`LHS \u003d (k(k+1) + 2(k+1)) / 2`\n\nNotice that `(k+1)` is a common factor in the numerator. Let\u0027s factor it out:\n`LHS \u003d (k+1)(k+2) / 2`\n\nWait a minute... this is **exactly the RHS of our goal!**\n\nWe have successfully started with the LHS for `k+1` and, by using our assumption for `k`, we have transformed it into the RHS for `k+1`. This proves the \"if-then\" statement.\n\n### Conclusion\n\nWe have proven:\n1.  The formula is true for `n\u003d1`.\n2.  If it\u0027s true for any number `k`, it must be true for `k+1`.\n\nThis creates a chain reaction. Because it\u0027s true for 1, it must be true for 2. Because it\u0027s true for 2, it must be true for 3. And so on, for all positive integers. The proof is complete.\n\nDoes breaking it down this way with a simpler formula make the roles of `k` and `k+1` clearer?",

Both are trying to achieve the same goal: **Use the assumption for `k` to prove the statement for `k+1`.**\n\n---\n\n### Inductive Step: Two Methods, Same Logic\n\n**Assumption (Inductive Hypothesis):** We assume `1 + 2 + ... + k \u003d k(k+1)/2` is true.\n\n| Method 1: The \"Substitution\" Method | Method 2: Your \"Add the Next Unit\" Method |\n| :--- | :--- |\n| **Goal:** Prove `1 + ... + (k+1) \u003d (k+1)(k+2)/2` | **Goal:** Prove `1 + ... + (k+1) \u003d (k+1)(k+2)/2` |\n| **1. Start with the LHS of the goal:** \u003cbr\u003e `1 + 2 + ... + k + (k+1)` | **1. Start with the entire assumption equation:** \u003cbr\u003e `1 + 2 + ... + k \u003d k(k+1)/2` |\n| **2. Isolate the part that matches the assumption:** \u003cbr\u003e `[1 + 2 + ... + k] + (k+1)` | **2. Add the next logical piece, `(k+1)`, to BOTH sides of the equation to keep it balanced:** \u003cbr\u003e `[1 + 2 + ... + k] + (k+1) \u003d [k(k+1)/2] + (k+1)`|\n| **3. We just need to simplify the right side.**|\n| **4. Do algebra on the resulting expression to match the goal\u0027s RHS.** | **4. Do algebra on the right side to match the goal\u0027s RHS.** |\n\n**The result is identical.** At step 3, both methods arrive at the exact same algebraic expression that needs to be simplified: `k(k+1)/2 + (k+1)`.\n\n### So why are you taught the first method? Why use `k`?\n\nYou have asked the most important question for understanding the formalism.\n\n1.  **Clarity of Logic:** The only reason we switch from `n` to `k` is to be extremely clear and avoid a logical trap. The statement we want to prove is \"For **ALL** `n`...\". If we use `n` in our assumption (\"Assume the formula works for `n`...\"), it sounds like we are assuming the very thing we are trying to prove. Using `k` makes it clear: we are assuming it\u0027s true for **ONE specific, but arbitrary** number `k` in order to prove it for the next one, `k+1`.\n\n2.  **It works for inequalities too:** Your \"add to both sides\" method works perfectly for equations. Since this is a product, the action is multiplication. We multiply both sides by `(1 + aₖ₊₁)`. Since it\u0027s positive, the `\u003e` sign doesn\u0027t change.\n`(1 + a₁)...(1 + aₖ)(1 + aₖ₊₁) \u003e (1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ)(1 + aₖ₊₁)`\n\n**Look familiar?** The expression on the right is *exactly* the expression you came up with in our previous discussion! You have already been using this more intuitive method in your head.\n\nFrom here, we just have to prove that the new Right-Hand Side is bigger than our goal\u0027s RHS:\n`(1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ)(1 + aₖ₊₁) \u003e 1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ + aₖ₊₁`\n\nAnd as we showed before, when you expand the left side, you get `1 + a₁ + ... + aₖ + aₖ₊₁` **plus** a bunch of extra positive terms, so the inequality is true.\n\n**Conclusion:** Your method is not only valid, it\u0027s excellent. It is the same logical process, just structured in a way that can feel more direct. The \"unuseful substitution\" is just a more formal way of writing down the exact same idea. In the second example, `10` was only a little bigger than `9`, but we subtracted a number `8` that was *much* bigger than `1`. The best way to learn the *structure* of an induction proof is to use it on a problem where the algebra is easy, so you can focus only on the logical steps.\n\nLet\u0027s prove a classic statement about divisibility.\n\n**The Problem:** Prove that for any integer `n ≥ 1`, the expression **`n³ - n` is always divisible by 3.**\n\nLet\u0027s test it first to see if it feels true.\n*   If n\u003d1: `1³ - 1 \u003d 0`. Is 0 divisible by 3? Yes (0 \u003d 3 × 0).\n*   If n\u003d2: `2³ - 2 \u003d 8 - 2 \u003d 6`. Is 6 divisible by 3? Yes (6 \u003d 3 × 2).\n*   If n\u003d3: `3³ - 3 \u003d 27 - 3 \u003d 24`. Is 24 divisible by 3? Yes (24 \u003d 3 × 8).\n*   If n\u003d4: `4³ - 4 \u003d 64 - 4 \u003d 60`. Is 60 divisible by 3? Yes (60 \u003d 3 × 20).\n\nThe pattern seems to hold. Now let\u0027s prove it with the formal three steps.\n\n---\n\n### **Step 1: Base Case (n\u003d1)**\n\nThis is the easy first step. We show the statement is true for the smallest possible value of `n`.\n*   Let `n\u003d1`.\n*   The expression is `1³ - 1 \u003d 0`.\n*   **Conclusion:** 0 is divisible by 3. The base case is true.\n\n---\n\n### **Step 2: The Inductive Hypothesis (Assume it\u0027s true for n\u003dk)**\n\nWe assume the statement is true for some random, arbitrary integer `k ≥ 1`. This assumption is our only tool for the next step.\n\n*   **We assume that `k³ - k` is divisible by 3.**\n\n(This is the most important part: \"is divisible by 3\" means we can write `k³ - k \u003d 3m` where `m` is some integer. This is our tool, written in a useful algebraic way).\n\n---\n\n### **Step 3: The Inductive Step (Prove it\u0027s true for n\u003dk+1)**\n\nThis is the main event. Our goal is to prove that **IF** our assumption for `k` is true, **THEN** it must also be true for the very next number, `k+1`.\n\n**Our Goal:** We need to prove that `(k+1)³ - (k+1)` is divisible by 3.\n\nLet\u0027s start with the expression and do some algebra.\n\n1.  **Expand the expression:**\n    `(k+1)³ - (k+1)`\n    The `(k+1)³` part expands to `k³ + 3k² + 3k + 1`.\n    So our full expression is:\n    `\u003d (k³ + 3k² + 3k + 1) - (k+1)`\n\n2.  **Simplify the expression:**\n    `\u003d k³ + 3k² + 3k + 1 - k - 1`\n    `\u003d k³ + 3k² + 2k`\n\n3.  **Use the Tool!** This is the magic step. We need to find our Inductive Hypothesis, `k³ - k`, hiding inside our new expression. Let\u0027s rearrange the terms to make it appear.\n    `\u003d (k³ - k) + 3k² + 3k`\n    *(Check the algebra: `k³ - k + 3k² + 3k` is the same as `k³ + 3k² + 2k`. It works!)*\n\n4.  **Substitute and Analyze:**\n    We assumed that `k³ - k` is divisible by 3, so we can replace it with `3m`.\n    `\u003d (3m) + 3k² + 3k`\n\n5.  **Draw the Final Conclusion:**\n    Now look at the result: `3m + 3k² + 3k`. We can factor a `3` out of every single term.\n    `\u003d 3(m + k² + k)`\n\nWe have successfully shown that the expression for `k+1` can be written as `3` times some integer (`m + k² + k`). By definition, this means the expression is **divisible by 3**.\n\n### Summary\n\nWe proved the statement is true for `n\u003d1`. Then we proved that if it\u0027s true for any number `k`, it must be true for `k+1`. This creates an unstoppable chain reaction. Because it\u0027s true for 1, it must be true for 2. Because it\u0027s true for 2, it must be true for 3, and so on, forever.\n\n**Proof complete.**\n\nDoes this example, with its different goal (divisibility instead of inequality), make the structure of the three steps clearer?",

      "text": "Of course. This is the final and most important step. We will create a perfect, clean LaTeX document that you can submit.\n\nThis document will do two things:\n1.  **It will show your thought process**, including the key questions you asked. This tells the teacher *how* you think, which is very valuable.\n2.  **It will present the final, formal proof** in a clean, step-by-step way.\n\nJust copy and paste the code below into a LaTeX editor (like Overleaf, or your own local editor), and it will produce a perfect PDF for you.\n\n---\n\n```latex\n\\documentclass[12pt, a4paper]{article}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{geometry}\n\\geometry{a4paper, margin\u003d1in}\n\\usepackage[slovene]{babel} % Added for proper Slovenian formatting\n\n\\pagestyle{empty}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\n% --- Header Information ---\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\textwidth}{l @{\\extracolsep{\\fill}} r}\n\\textbf{FKKT UM, Matematika A} \u0026 \\textbf{1. domača naloga} \\hspace{1cm} \\textbf{2025/2026} \\\\\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\vspace{0.5cm}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\textwidth}{l @{\\extracolsep{\\fill}} r}\n\\underline{Ime in priimek\\hspace{5cm}} \u0026 \\underline{K\\hspace{1cm} KI\\hspace{1cm}} \\\\\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n% --- Problem Statement ---\n\\textbf{1. Naj bo $a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$ za vsak $i \\in \\mathbb{N}$. S pomočjo matematične indukcije dokaži, da za vsak $n \\in \\mathbb{N}, n \\geq 2$, velja}\n\\[\n(1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_n) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_n\n\\]\n\\textbf{Vsak korak utemelji!}\n\\vspace{1cm}\n\n% --- Section for Thought Process ---\n\\section*{Miselni proces reševanja}\n\nPreden sem se lotil formalnega dokaza, sem želel razumeti, zakaj je trditev sploh resnična. Opazil sem, da je leva stran (LHS) produkt, desna stran (RHS) pa vsota. Pri razčlenjevanju produkta na levi strani bodo očitno nastali dodatni \"mešani\" členi (kot npr. $a_1a_2$), ki jih na desni strani ni. Ker so vsi $a_i$ pozitivni, sem predvideval, da so ti dodatni členi razlog za neenakost.\n\nKljučno vprašanje pri indukcijskem koraku je bilo, kako uporabiti indukcijsko predpostavko, ki je neenakost ($\u003e$) in ne enačba ($\u003d$). Moj prvi razmislek je bil, da bi nekako \"odštel\" predpostavko od cilja, a sem ugotovil, da odštevanje neenakosti ni veljavna operacija.\n\nPravi preboj se je zgodil, ko sem dojel strategijo \"logičnega mostu\":\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item Začnem z levo stranjo cilja za $n\u003dk+1$.\n    \\item Uporabim indukcijsko predpostavko, da del te leve strani zamenjam z nečim \\textbf{manjšim}.\n    \\item Nato z navadno algebro dokažem, da je ta nov, manjši izraz še vedno večji od desne strani cilja.\n\\end{enumerate}\nS tem ustvarim neprekinjeno verigo neenakosti: $\\text{LHS}_{k+1} \u003e \\text{vmesni izraz} \u003e \\text{RHS}_{k+1}$, kar dokaže trditev.\n\n% --- Section for the Formal Proof ---\n\\section*{Formalni dokaz z matematično indukcijo}\n\n\\subsection*{1. korak: Baza indukcije ($n\u003d2$)}\nPreverimo veljavnost trditve za najmanjši $n$, to je $n\u003d2$:\n\\[ (1+a_1)(1+a_2) \u003e 1+a_1+a_2 \\]\nRazčlenimo levo stran:\n\\[ 1 + a_1 + a_2 + a_1a_2 \u003e 1+a_1+a_2 \\]\nOd obeh strani odštejemo $1+a_1+a_2$:\n\\[ a_1a_2 \u003e 0 \\]\nKer je po predpostavki naloge $a_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^+$ za vsak $i$, sta $a_1$ in $a_2$ pozitivni števili. Produkt dveh pozitivnih števil je vedno pozitiven. S tem je baza indukcije dokazana in trditev velja za $n\u003d2$.\n\n\\subsection*{2. korak: Indukcijska predpostavka ($n\u003dk$)}\nPredpostavimo, da trditev velja za neko poljubno naravno število $k \\ge 2$. To pomeni, da predpostavljamo veljavnost naslednje neenakosti (to je naše orodje):\n\\[ (1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k \\]\n\n\\subsection*{3. korak: Indukcijski korak (dokaz za $n\u003dk+1$)}\nDokazati moramo, da iz predpostavke sledi veljavnost trditve za $n\u003dk+1$. Naš cilj je dokazati:\n\\[ (1 + a_1)\\dots(1 + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \u003e 1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1} \\]\n\nZačnemo z levo stranjo (LHS) ciljne neenakosti:\n\\[ \\text{LHS} \u003d (1 + a_1)(1 + a_2)\\dots(1 + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \\]\nSedaj uporabimo indukcijsko predpostavko. Prvi del izraza, $(1 + a_1)\\dots(1 + a_k)$, je večji od $1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k$. Zato lahko ta del zamenjamo z manjšim izrazom, s čimer ustvarimo neenakost:\n\\[ \\text{LHS} \u003e (1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \\]\nSedaj moramo samo še dokazati, da je ta nov izraz na desni strani večji od desne strani našega cilja. Uporabimo distributivnost:\n\\[ (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \u003d (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) \\cdot 1 + (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k) \\cdot a_{k+1} \\]\n\\[ \u003d 1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1} + a_1a_{k+1} + a_2a_{k+1} + \\dots + a_ka_{k+1} \\]\nPreuredimo člene, da prepoznamo desno stran našega cilja:\n\\[ \u003d \\underbrace{(1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1})}_{\\text{RHS našega cilja}} + \\underbrace{(a_1a_{k+1} + a_2a_{k+1} + \\dots + a_ka_{k+1})}_{\\text{\"Dodatni členi\"}} \\]\nKer so vsi $a_i \u003e 0$, je vsak produkt $a_ia_{k+1}$ pozitiven. Vsota teh pozitivnih produktov je prav tako pozitivna. To pomeni:\n\\[ (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \u003e 1 + a_1 + a_2 + \\dots + a_k + a_{k+1} \\]\nS tem smo zgradili celotno verigo:\n\\[ (1 + a_1)\\dots(1+a_{k+1}) \\quad \u003e \\quad (1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_k)(1+a_{k+1}) \\quad \u003e \\quad 1 + a_1 + \\dots + a_{k+1} \\]\nKer je prvi člen v verigi strogo večji od zadnjega, je indukcijski korak dokazan.\n\n\\subsection*{Zaključek}\nKer velja baza indukcije in ker smo dokazali, da iz veljavnosti trditve za $n\u003dk$ sledi njena veljavnost za $n\u003dk+1$, po principu matematične indukcije trditev velja za vsa naravna števila $n \\ge 2$.\n\\hfill \\textbf{Q.E.D.}\n\n\\end{document}\n```",

To pomeni, da mora biti tudi desna stran enačbe, `8 - 2x`, nujno nenegativna. Če bi bila negativna, enačba ne bi imela rešitev, saj pozitivno število ne more biti enako negativnemu. Vsako dobljeno rešitev bom nato preveril s pogojem iz prvega koraka. Samo tiste rešitve, ki ustrezajo pogoju, so pravilne rešitve prvotne enačbe.\n\nPojdimo po vrsti.\n\n---\n\n### **Rešitev naloge**\n\n**2. Reši enačbo**\n\\[ |x^2 + 5x| \u003d 8 - 2x \\]\n\n**1. korak: Določitev pogoja**\n\nKer je leva stran enačbe po definiciji absolutne vrednosti vedno nenegativna, mora to veljati tudi za desno stran:\n\\[ 8 - 2x \\geq 0 \\]\nRešimo neenačbo:\n\\[ 8 \\geq 2x \\]\n\\[ 4 \\geq x \\]\n\\[ x \\leq 4 \\]\n**Pogoj:** Vse rešitve, ki jih bomo dobili, morajo biti manjše ali enake 4.\n\n**2. korak: Razdelitev na dva primera**\n\nOdpravimo absolutno vrednost tako, da enačbo razdelimo na dve možnosti:\n\n**a) Primer 1: `x² + 5x \u003d 8 - 2x`**\nTo je standardna kvadratna enačba. Vse člene prenesemo na levo stran, da dobimo enačbo v obliki `ax² + bx + c \u003d 0`.\n\\[ x^2 + 5x + 2x - 8 \u003d 0 \\]\n\\[ x^2 + 7x - 8 \u003d 0 \\]\nTo enačbo lahko enostavno razstavimo z Vietovim pravilom (iščemo dve števili, katerih produkt je -8 in vsota 7. To sta 8 in -1).\n\\[ (x+8)(x-1) \u003d 0 \\]\nDobimo dve možni rešitvi:\n*   `x₁ \u003d -8`\n*   `x₂ \u003d 1`\n\n**b) Primer 2: `x² + 5x \u003d -(8 - 2x)`**\nNajprej odpravimo oklepaj na desni strani.\n\\[ x^2 + 5x \u003d -8 + 2x \\]\nVse člene prenesemo na levo stran.\n\\[ x^2 + 5x - 2x + 8 \u003d 0 \\]\n\\[ x^2 + 3x + 8 \u003d 0 \\]\nTo je kvadratna enačba. Da, je. **Tudi ta rešitev je veljavna.**\n\n**Zaključek:**\nEnačba ima dve rešitvi. Množica rešitev je:\n\\[ \\mathcal{R} \u003d \\{-8, 1\\} \\]",

      "text": "Seveda, lotiva se skiciranja grafa te funkcije. To je odličen primer, ker združuje absolutne vrednosti in racionalno funkcijo (ulomek). Rešitev je sistematična in jo bova razdelila na logične korake.\n\n### **Miselni proces in strategija**\n\n1.  **Definicijsko območje:** Najprej morava ugotoviti, kje funkcija sploh obstaja. Ker imamo ulomek, imenovalec ne sme biti enak 0. To nama bo dalo navpične asimptote.\n2.  **Zapis funkcije po odsekih:** Dve absolutni vrednosti pomenita, da se bo predpis za funkcijo spreminjal. \"Kritične točke\", kjer se izrazi znotraj absolutnih vrednosti spremenijo, so `x\u003d0` (za `|x|`) in `x\u003d-2` (za `|x+2|`). Te točke nama razdelijo realno os na tri intervale. Za vsak interval bova zapisala poenostavljeno obliko funkcije brez absolutnih vrednosti.\n3.  **Ničle funkcije:** Ugotovila bova, kje graf seka os x. To se zgodi, ko je števec enak 0.\n4.  **Začetna vrednost:** Ugotovila bova, kje graf seka os y, tako da bova izračunala `f(0)`.\n5.  **Asimptote:** Navpične sva že določila pri definicijskem območju. Preveriti morava še, ali obstaja vodoravna asimptota, tako da pogledava, kaj se dogaja s funkcijo, ko gre `x` proti `+∞` in `-∞`.\n6.  **Skica grafa:** Z vsemi zbranimi informacijami (definicijsko območje, poenostavljeni predpisi, ničle, asimptote) bova lahko natančno narisala graf.\n\n---\n\n### **Rešitev naloge**\n\nDana je funkcija:\n\\[ f(x) \u003d \\frac{|x|}{|x+2| - 1} \\]\n\n**1. Definicijsko območje (D\u003csub\u003ef\u003c/sub\u003e)**\n\nFunkcija ni definirana, ko je imenovalec enak 0.\n\\[ |x+2| - 1 \u003d 0 \\]\n\\[ |x+2| \u003d 1 \\]\nTo se zgodi v dveh primerih:\n1.  `x + 2 \u003d 1   \u003d\u003e   x \u003d -1`\n2.  `x + 2 \u003d -1  \u003d\u003e   x \u003d -3`\n\nDefinicijsko območje so torej vsa realna števila razen -1 in -3.\n\\[ D_f \u003d \\mathbb{R} \\setminus \\{-3, -1\\} \\]\nPri `x \u003d -3` in `x \u003d -1` ima funkcija navpični asimptoti.\n\n**2. Zapis funkcije po odsekih**\n\nKritični točki sta `x \u003d -2` in `x \u003d 0`. Realno os razdelimo na tri intervale.\n\n**a) Interval 1: `x \u003c -2`**\nNa tem območju je `x` negativen in `x+2` je negativen.\n*   `|x| \u003d -x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d -(x+2) \u003d -x-2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n\\[ f(x) \u003d \\frac{-x}{(-x-2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{-x}{-x-3} \u003d \\frac{x}{x+3} \\]\n\n**b) Interval 2: `-2 ≤ x \u003c 0`**\nNa tem območju je `x` negativen, `x+2` pa je pozitiven.\n*   `|x| \u003d -x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d x+2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n\\[ f(x) \u003d \\frac{-x}{(x+2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{-x}{x+1} \\]\n\n**c) Interval 3: `x ≥ 0`**\nNa tem območju sta `x` in `x+2` pozitivna.\n*   `|x| \u003d x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d x+2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n\\[ f(x) \u003d \\frac{x}{(x+2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{x}{x+1} \\]\n\n**3. Ničle in presečišče z y-osjo**\n\n*   **Ničle (`f(x)\u003d0`):** Ulomek je enak 0, ko je števec enak 0.\n    `|x| \u003d 0   \u003d\u003e   x \u003d 0`\n    Funkcija ima eno ničlo pri `x\u003d0`. To je točka **(0, 0)**.\n\n*   **Presečišče z y-osjo (`f(0)`):** Ker je ničla pri `x\u003d0`, je to tudi edino presečišče z y-osjo. Točka je **(0, 0)**.\n\n**4. Asimptote**\n\n*   **Navpični asimptoti:** Kot sva že ugotovila, sta pri `x \u003d -3` in `x \u003d -1`.\n\n*   **Vodoravna asimptota:**\n    *   Ko gre `x → ∞` (uporabimo predpis za `x ≥ 0`):\n        \\[ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{x}{x+1} \u003d 1 \\]\n    *   Ko gre `x → -∞` (uporabimo predpis za `x \u003c -2`):\n        \\[ \\lim_{x \\to -\\infty} \\frac{x}{x+3} \u003d 1 \\]\n    Vodoravna asimptota je **`y \u003d 1`**.\n\n**5. Povzetek za risanje grafa**\n\n*   **Navpični asimptoti:** `x \u003d -3`, `x \u003d -1`\n*   **Vodoravna asimptota:** `y \u003d 1`\n*   **Ničla/presečišče:** `(0, 0)`\n*   **Predpisi po delih:**\n    *   Za `x ∈ (-∞, -2)`:  `f(x) \u003d x / (x+3)`\n    *   Za `x ∈ [-2, 0)`:    `f(x) \u003d -x / (x+1)`\n    *   Za `x ∈ [0, ∞)`:     `f(x) \u003d x / (x+1)`\n\n**6. Skiciranje grafa**\n\n1.  Narišemo koordinatni sistem.\n2.  Z črtkanimi črtami vrišemo asimptote: navpični premici pri `x\u003d-3` in `x\u003d-1` ter vodoravno premico pri `y\u003d1`.\n3.  Označimo edino točko, ki jo poznamo: `(0, 0)`.\n4.  **Narišemo graf po delih:**\n    *   **Desno od `x\u003d0`:** Rišemo graf `y \u003d x/(x+1)`. Začne se pri `(0,0)` in se približuje vodoravni asimptoti `y\u003d1`, ko gre `x` proti neskončnosti.\n    *   **Med `x\u003d-2` in `x\u003d0`:** Rišemo graf `y \u003d -x/(x+1)`.\n        *   Pri `x\u003d0` je vrednost 0.\n        *   Pri `x\u003d-2` izračunamo vrednost: `f(-2) \u003d -(-2)/(-2+1) \u003d 2/(-1) \u003d -2`. Točka `(-2, -2)` je \"lomna točka\" grafa.\n        *   Ko se `x` približuje `-1` z desne strani, gre `y` proti `+∞`.\n    *   **Levo od `x\u003d-2`:** Rišemo graf `y \u003d x/(x+3)`.\n        *   Graf se začne pri točki `(-2, -2)`.\n        *   Ko se `x` približuje `-3` z leve strani, gre `y` proti `+∞`.\n        *   Ko se `x` približuje `-3` z desne strani, gre `y` proti `-∞`.\n        *   Ko gre `x` proti `-∞`, se graf približuje vodoravni asimptoti `y\u003d1` od zgoraj.\n\nS temi informacijami lahko skiciramo celoten graf, ki bo sestavljen iz treh ločenih krivulj.ulj.",

Legitimna izpeljava limite je ključna za pravilen dokaz.\n\nPoglejva si izračun vodoravne asimptote formalno, korak za korakom.\n\nVodoravno asimptoto določimo tako, da izračunamo limito funkcije, ko gre `x` proti `+∞` in ko gre `x` proti `-∞`.\n\n---\n\n### **1. Limita, ko gre `x → +∞`**\n\nKo `x` postane zelo veliko pozitivno število, je zagotovo v območju `x ≥ 0`. Zato moramo uporabiti predpis za ta interval:\n\\[ f(x) \u003d \\frac{x}{x+1} \\]\nSedaj izračunamo limito:\n\\[ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{x}{x+1} \\]\nKer gre števec proti `∞` in imenovalec proti `∞`, imamo nedoločeno obliko `∞/∞`. Standardni postopek za reševanje takšne limite je, da celoten ulomek (števec in imenovalec) delimo z najvišjo potenco `x` v imenovalcu, kar je v tem primeru `x¹` oziroma `x`.\n\n\\[ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{\\frac{x}{x}}{\\frac{x+1}{x}} \\]\nPoenostavimo oba dela ulomka:\n\\[ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{1}{\\frac{x}{x} + \\frac{1}{x}} \\]\n\\[ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{1}{1 + \\frac{1}{x}} \\]\nSedaj lahko izračunamo limito.       "text": "Seveda, popolnoma razumem. Oprostite za nejasnost. Tukaj je celotna rešitev, zapisana v jasnem in berljivem formatu, brez LaTeX kode.\n\n---\n\n### **5. Funkcija f je podana s predpisom**\n\n$$ f(x) \u003d \\frac{|x|}{|x+2| - 1} $$\n\n**Skiciraj graf funkcije f.**\n\n---\n\n### **Miselni proces in strategija**\n\n1.  **Definicijsko območje:** Najprej moram ugotoviti, kje funkcija sploh obstaja. Ker imam ulomek, imenovalec ne sme biti enak 0. To mi bo dalo navpične asimptote.\n2.  **Zapis funkcije po odsekih:** Dve absolutni vrednosti pomenita, da se bo predpis za funkcijo spreminjal. \"Kritične točke\", kjer se izrazi znotraj absolutnih vrednosti spremenijo, so `x\u003d0` (za `|x|`) in `x\u003d-2` (za `|x+2|`). Te točke mi razdelijo realno os na tri intervale. Za vsak interval bom zapisal poenostavljeno obliko funkcije brez absolutnih vrednosti.\n3.  **Ničle funkcije:** Ugotovil bom, kje graf seka os x. To se zgodi, ko je števec enak 0.\n4.  **Začetna vrednost:** Ugotovil bom, kje graf seka os y, tako da bom izračunal `f(0)`.\n5.  **Asimptote:** Navpične sem že določil pri definicijskem območju. Preveriti moram še, ali obstaja vodoravna asimptota, tako da pogledam, kaj se dogaja s funkcijo, ko gre `x` proti `+∞` in `-∞`.\n6.  **Skica grafa:** Z vsemi zbranimi informacijami (definicijsko območje, poenostavljeni predpisi, ničle, asimptote) bom lahko natančno narisal graf.\n\n---\n\n### **Rešitev naloge**\n\n#### **1. Definicijsko območje (D\u003csub\u003ef\u003c/sub\u003e)**\n\nFunkcija ni definirana, ko je imenovalec enak 0.\n$ |x+2| - 1 \u003d 0 $\n$ |x+2| \u003d 1 $\n\nTo se zgodi v dveh primerih:\n1.  `x + 2 \u003d 1   \u003d\u003e   x \u003d -1`\n2.  `x + 2 \u003d -1  \u003d\u003e   x \u003d -3`\n\nDefinicijsko območje so torej vsa realna števila razen -1 in -3.\n$ D_f \u003d ℝ \\setminus \\{-3, -1\\} $\nPri `x \u003d -3` in `x \u003d -1` ima funkcija **navpični asimptoti**.\n\n#### **2. Zapis funkcije po odsekih**\n\nKritični točki sta `x \u003d -2` in `x \u003d 0`. Realno os razdelimo na tri intervale.\n\n**a) Interval 1: `x \u003c -2`**\nNa tem območju je `x` negativen in `x+2` je negativen.\n*   `|x| \u003d -x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d -(x+2) \u003d -x-2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n$$ f(x) \u003d \\frac{-x}{(-x-2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{-x}{-x-3} \u003d \\frac{x}{x+3} $$\n\n**b) Interval 2: `-2 ≤ x \u003c 0`**\nNa tem območju je `x` negativen, `x+2` pa je pozitiven.\n*   `|x| \u003d -x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d x+2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n$$ f(x) \u003d \\frac{-x}{(x+2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{-x}{x+1} $$\n\n**c) Interval 3: `x ≥ 0`**\nNa tem območju sta `x` in `x+2` pozitivna.\n*   `|x| \u003d x`\n*   `|x+2| \u003d x+2`\n\nVstavimo v funkcijo:\n$$ f(x) \u003d \\frac{x}{(x+2) - 1} \u003d \\frac{x}{x+1} $$\n\n#### **3. Ničle in presečišče z y-osjo**\n\n*   **Ničle (`f(x)\u003d0`):** Ulomek je enak 0, ko je števec enak 0.\n    `|x| \u003d 0   \u003d\u003e   x \u003d 0`\n    Funkcija ima eno ničlo pri `x\u003d0`. To je točka **(0, 0)**.\n\n*   **Presečišče z y-osjo (`f(0)`):** Ker je ničla pri `x\u003d0`, je to tudi edino presečišče z y-osjo. Točka je **(0, 0)**.\n\n#### **4. Asimptote**\n\n*   **Navpični asimptoti:** Kot smo že ugotovili, sta pri `x \u003d -3` in `x \u003d -1`.\n\n*   **Vodoravna asimptota:**\n\n    *   **Ko gre `x → +∞`** (uporabimo predpis za `x ≥ 0`):\n        $$ \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{x}{x+1} \u003d \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{\\frac{x}{x}}{\\frac{x}{x}+\\frac{1}{x}} \u003d \\lim_{x \\to \\infty} \\frac{1}{1+\\frac{1}{x}} \u003d \\frac{1}{1+0} \u003d 1 $$\n\n    *   **Ko gre `x → -∞`** (uporabimo predpis za `x \u003c -2`):\n        $$ \\lim_{x \\to -\\infty} \\frac{x}{x+3} \u003d \\lim_{x \\to -\\infty} \\frac{\\frac{x}{x}}{\\frac{x}{x}+\\frac{3}{x}} \u003d \\lim_{x \\to -\\infty} \\frac{1}{1+\\frac{3}{x}} \u003d \\frac{1}{1+0} \u003d 1 $$\n\n    Vodoravna asimptota je **`y \u003d 1`**.\n\n#### **5. Razlog: To je \"kritična točka\" absolutne vrednosti\n\n\"Lomne točke\" na grafih funkcij z absolutnimi vrednostmi se **vedno** zgodijo na mestih, kjer je izraz znotraj absolutne vrednosti enak nič. Zakaj? Ker je to točno tisto mesto, kjer se \"pravilo\" za funkcijo spremeni.\n\nV tvoji funkciji imaš dve absolutni vrednosti:\n*   `|x|`: Kritična točka je `x\u003d0`.\n*   `|x+2|`: Kritična točka je `x+2 \u003d 0`, torej `x\u003d-2`.\n\nTe točke, `x\u003d-2` in `x\u003d0`, so meje med našimi intervali. So točke, kjer se graf \"sešije\" skupaj iz različnih delov, ki so definirani z različnimi enačbami.\n\n*   **Levo od x\u003d-2** smo uporabili enačbo `f(x) \u003d x / (x+3)`.\n*   **Desno od x\u003d-2** (do 0) smo uporabili enačbo `f(x) \u003d -x / (x+1)`.\n\nKer se pri `x\u003d-2` srečata dva popolnoma različna grafa (graf funkcije `x/(x+3)` in graf funkcije `-x/(x+1)`), je zelo verjetno, da se na stičišču ne bosta \"lepo\" zlila skupaj, ampak bosta tvorila oster kot oziroma \"lom\". **Točka `(-2, -2)` je dobesedno stičišče dveh različnih krivulj.**\n\n### 2. Njegov odvod (s pravilom za kvocient) je:\n    `f\u0027(x) \u003d 3 / (x+3)²`\n    Naklon v točki, ki je infinitezimalno blizu -2 z leve strani, je:\n    `f\u0027(-2) \u003d 3 / (-2+3)² \u003d 3 / 1² \u003d 3`\n    **Torej, tik pred točko -2 se graf vzpenja z naklonom 3.**\n\n*   **Naklon z desne strani (za x \u003e -2):**\n    Uporabimo predpis `f(x) \u003d -x / (x+1)`.
